Geometric orbital integrals and the center of the enveloping algebra Thanks: The authors are much indebted to Laurent Clozel for his stimulating remarks during the preparation of the paper, and for reading the preliminary version of this paper very carefully.
Jean-Michel Bismut, Shu Shen

Abstract
. The purpose of this paper is to extend the explicit geometric evaluation of semisimple orbital integrals for smooth kernels for the Casimir operator obtained by the first author to the case of kernels for arbitrary elements in the center of the enveloping algebra.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
11F72, 22E30,

1.
Introduction

In
[
B11
, Chapter 6]
, one of us established a geometric
formula for the semisimple orbital integrals of smooth kernels associated with the Casimir. The purpose of
this paper is to extend this formula to the smooth kernels where more
general elements of
the center of the enveloping algebra also appear.

Let us briefly describe our main
result in more detail.
Let
G
be a connected real reductive group, and let
\mathfrak{g}
be
its Lie algebra. Let
\theta\in\mathrm{Aut}\left(G\right)
be a
Cartan involution, and let
K\subset G
be the corresponding maximal
compact subgroup with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{k}
. Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{p}\oplus\mathfrak{k}
be the associated Cartan splitting.
Let
B
be a symmetric nondegenerate bilinear form on
\mathfrak{g}

which is
G
and
\theta
invariant, positive on

\mathfrak{p}
and negative on
\mathfrak{k}
. Let
X=G/K
be the associated symmetric space, a
Riemannian manifold with parallel nonpositive curvature.

Let
\rho^{E}:K\to U\left(E\right)
be a finite-dimensional unitary
representation of
K
, and let
F=G\times_{K}E
be the corresponding
vector bundle on
X
. Then
G
acts on the left on
C^{\infty}\left(X,F\right)
. Let
U\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
be the enveloping
algebra of
\mathfrak{g}
, and let
Z\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
be the
center of
U\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
. Then
Z\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
acts on
C^{\infty}\left(X,F\right)
and its action
commutes with the left action of
G
. Among the elements of

Z\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
, there is the Casimir
C^{\mathfrak{g}}
,
whose action on
C^{\infty}\left(X,F\right)
is denoted

C^{\mathfrak{g},X}
.

Let

\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{even}}\left(\mathbf{R}\right)
denote the even real
functions on
\mathbf{R}
that lie in the Schwartz space

\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbf{R}\right)
. Let
\mu\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{even}}\left(\mathbf{R}\right)
be such that if

\widehat{\mu}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{even}}\left(\mathbf{R}\right)
is its Fourier transform,
there is
C>0
, and for any
k\in\mathbf{N}
, there is
c_{k}>0
such that

(1.1)

\left|\widehat{\mu}^{(k)}\left(y\right)\right|\leq c_{k}\exp\left(-Cy^{2}\right).

If
A\in\mathbf{R}
,
\mu\left(\sqrt{C^{\mathfrak{g},X}+A}\right)
is a
well-defined operator with a smooth kernel.

If
\gamma\in G
is semisimple, as explained in
[
B11
, Section 6.2]
,
the orbital integral

\mathrm{Tr}^{\left[\gamma\right]}\left[\mu\left(\sqrt{C^{\mathfrak{g},X}+A}\right)\right]
is well-defined, and it only depends on the
conjugacy class of
\gamma
in
G
. After conjugation, we can write

\gamma
in the form
\gamma=e^{a}k^{-1},a\in\mathfrak{p},k\in K,\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{-1}\right)a=a
. If
Z\left(\gamma\right)\subset G
is the
centralizer of
\gamma
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)
, then
\theta
acts on
Z\left(\gamma\right)
,
and
Z\left(\gamma\right)
is a possibly nonconnected reductive
group. Let
\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)=\mathfrak{p}\left(\gamma\right)\oplus\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
be the
associated Cartan splitting.

Let

I^{\cdot}\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
be the algebra of invariant
polynomials on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, and let

\tau_{\mathrm{D}}:I^{\cdot}\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)\simeq Z\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
denote the Duflo
isomorphism
[
Du70
, Théorème V.2]
. If
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
is
a Cartan subalgebra, let
I^{\cdot}\left(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}\right)
denote the algebra of polynomials on
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
that
are invariant under the corresponding algebraic Weyl
group, so that
we have the canonical identification
I^{\cdot}\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)\simeq I^{\cdot}\left(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}\right)
1
1

1

This
isomorphism is usually written in its complex version

I^{\cdot}\left(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)\simeq I^{\cdot}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}},\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
.
In Subsections
3.3
and
6.3
, the corresponding real version is
derived. Such considerations will also apply to other complex
isomorphisms.
.
There is a Harish-Chandra isomorphism

\phi_{\mathrm{HC}}:Z\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)\simeq I^{\cdot}\left(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}\right)
. By

[
Du70
, Lemme V.1]
, the Duflo and
Harish-Chandra isomorphisms are known to be compatible.

There is a canonical projection
\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)
2
2

2

This projection is defined in
Subsection
8.2
.
, that induces a corresponding projection

I^{\cdot}\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)\to I^{\cdot}\left(\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)\right)
. If
L\in Z\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
,
let

L^{\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)}
denote the differential
operator on
\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)
canonically associated
with the projection of
\tau_{\mathrm{D}}^{-1}L
on
I^{\cdot}\left(\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)\right)
. In particular, up to a constant,
-\left(C^{\mathfrak{g}}\right)^{\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)}
extends to the standard
Laplacian on the Euclidean vector space

\mathfrak{z}_{i}\left(\gamma\right)=\mathfrak{p}\left(\gamma\right)\oplus i\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
.

Following
[
B11
, Chapter 5]
, in Definition
2.6
, we define a smooth function

\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}:i\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)\to\mathbf{C}
. Let

\delta_{a}
be the Dirac mass at
a\in\mathfrak{p}\left(\gamma\right)
. Then

\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)\mathrm{Tr}^{E}\left[\rho^{E}\left(k^{-1}e^{-Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}\right)\right]\otimes\delta_{a}

is a distribution on
\mathfrak{z}_{i}\left(\gamma\right)
.
3
3

3

In
the sequel,
\otimes
will be omitted.

Our main result, which is repeated in the text as Theorem

9.1
is as follows.

Theorem 1.1
.

The following identity holds:

(1.2)

\mathrm{Tr}^{\left[\gamma\right]}\left[L\mu\left(\sqrt{C^{\mathfrak{g},X}+A}\right)\right]\\
=L^{\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)}\mu\left(\sqrt{\left(C^{\mathfrak{g}}\right)^{\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)}+A}\right)\left[\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)\mathrm{Tr}^{E}\left[\rho^{E}\left(k^{-1}e^{-Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}\right)\right]\delta_{a}\right]\left(0\right).

When
L=1
, our theorem was already established in

[
B11
, Theorem 6.2.2]
.

The proofs in
[
B11
]
used a construction of a new object,
the hypoelliptic Laplacian. Here, we will only need the results of

[
B11
]
.

Our proof is done in two steps. In a first step, using the
results of
[
B11
]
, we prove Theorem

1.1
when
\gamma\in G
is regular. In this case,
using the properties of the Harish-Chandra isomorphism

[
HC66
]
, the proof is
relatively easy.

When
\gamma
is nonregular, we combine our result for
\gamma

regular with limit arguments due to Harish-Chandra on the behavior of
orbital integrals when
\gamma^{\prime}
regular converges to
\gamma
.
In both steps, remarkable and nontrivial properties of the function

\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}
are used. One of these properties is that
one essential piece of
\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}
can be calculated
only in terms of imaginary roots.

This paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we
describe the geometric setting, and we explain the formula for the
semisimple orbital integrals that was obtained in
[
B11
]
.

In Section
3
, we recall some of the properties of Cartan
subalgebras, Cartan subgroups, and of the corresponding root systems.

In Section
4
, we express the restriction of the function

\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}
to Cartan subalgebras in terms of a positive root
system.

In Section
5
, we specialize the results of the previous
section to the case where
\gamma
is regular. We prove a crucial and unexpected
smooth dependence of
\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}
on
\gamma
.

In Section
6
, we explain in some detail the
Harish-Chandra isomorphism.

In Section
7
, we establish Theorem
1.1

when
\gamma
is regular.

In Section
8
, when
\gamma
is non necessarily
regular, we study the limit of
\mathcal{J}_{\gamma^{\prime}}
, and the limit of
our formula for regular orbital integrals as
\gamma^{\prime}

regular converges to
\gamma
in a suitable sense.

In Section
9
, using the results of the
previous section, we establish Theorem
1.1
in full
generality.

Finally, in Section
10
, we prove that our formula is
compatible to the index theory for Dirac operators, and also with
known results on Dirac cohomology
[
HuP02
]
.

2.
Geometric formulas for orbital integrals and the Casimir

In this Section, we explain the geometric formula
given in
[
B11
, Chapter 6]
for the
semisimple orbital integrals associated with the proper smooth
kernels for the Casimir.

This section is organized as follows. In Subsection

2.1
, we introduce the real reductive group
G
, its
maximal compact subgroup
K
, the Lie
algebras
\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k}
, and the
symmetric space
X=G/K
.

In Subsection
2.2
, we recall the definition of
semisimple elements in
G
, and of the corresponding displacement
function.

In Subsection
2.3
, we introduce the enveloping algebra

U\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
, and the Casimir element
C^{\mathfrak{g}}\in U\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
.

In Subsection
2.4
, given a unitary representation of

K
, we construct the corresponding vector bundle
F
on
X
, and the elliptic operator

C^{\mathfrak{g},X}
which is
just the action of
C^{\mathfrak{g}}
on
C^{\infty}\left(X,F\right)
.

In Subsection
2.5
, given
\mu\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{even}}\left(\mathbf{R}\right)
such that its Fourier transform has the
proper Gaussian decay, if
A\in\mathbf{R}
, we recall the definition of the semisimple orbital
integrals associated with the smooth kernel for

\mu\left(\sqrt{C^{\mathfrak{g},X}+A}\right)
. Among these kernels,
there is the heat kernel for
C^{\mathfrak{g},X}
.

In Subsection
2.6
, if
\gamma\in G
is semisimple, if

Z\left(\gamma\right)\subset G
is its centralizer with Lie algebra

\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)
, if
\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)

is the compact part of
\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)
, we recall the definition of the
function
\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}
on
i\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
given in
[
B11
, Theorem 5.5.1]
.

In Subsection
2.7
, we study the behavior of

\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}

when replacing by
\gamma
by
\gamma^{-1}
,
and also by complex conjugation.

Finally, in Subsection
2.8
, we state the geometric
formula obtained in
[
B11
]
for the above
orbital integrals, in which the function
\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}
plays
a key role.

2.1.
Reductive groups and symmetric spaces

Let
G
be a connected reductive real Lie group, and let

\mathfrak{g}
be its Lie algebra. Let

\theta\in\mathrm{Aut}\left(G\right)
be a
Cartan involution. Then
\theta
acts as an automorphism of

\mathfrak{g}
. Let

K\subset G
be the fixed point set of
\theta
. Then
K
is a
compact connected subgroup of
G
, which is a maximal compact
subgroup.
If

\mathfrak{k}\subset\mathfrak{g}
is the Lie algebra of

K
, then

\mathfrak{k}
is the fixed point set of
\theta
in

\mathfrak{g}
. Let

\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathfrak{g}
be the eigenspace of
\theta
corresponding to
the eigenvalue
-1
, so that we have the Cartan decomposition

(2.1)

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{p}\oplus\mathfrak{k}.

Put

(2.2)

\displaystyle m=\dim\mathfrak{p},

\displaystyle n=\dim\mathfrak{k},

so that

(2.3)

\dim\mathfrak{g}=m+n.

Let

B
be a
G
and
\theta
invariant
bilinear symmetric nondegenerate form on
\mathfrak{g}
. Then
(
2.1
) is a
B
-orthogonal splitting. We assume that
B
is positive on

\mathfrak{p}
and negative on
\mathfrak{k}
. Let
\left\langle\,\right\rangle=-B\left(\cdot,\theta\cdot\right)
be the corresponding
scalar product on
\mathfrak{g}
. Let
B^{*}
be the bilinear
symmetric form on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}=\mathfrak{p}^{*}\oplus\mathfrak{k}^{*}
which is dual to
B
.

Let
\omega^{\mathfrak{g}}
be the canonical left-invariant
1
-form on

G
with values in
\mathfrak{g}
. By (
2.1
),

\omega^{\mathfrak{g}}
splits as

(2.4)

\omega^{\mathfrak{g}}=\omega^{\mathfrak{p}}+\omega^{\mathfrak{k}}.

Let

X=G/K
be the corresponding symmetric space. Then
p:G\to X=G/K

is a
K
-principal bundle, and
\omega^{\mathfrak{k}}
is a
connection form. Also the tangent bundle

TX
is given by

(2.5)

TX=G\times_{K}\mathfrak{p}.

Then
TX
is equipped with the scalar product
\left\langle\,\right\rangle
induced by
B
, so that
X
is a Riemannian
manifold. The connection
\nabla^{TX}
on
TX
which is induced by

\omega^{\mathfrak{k}}
is the Levi-Civita connection of
TX
, and its
curvature is parallel and nonpositive. Also
G
acts isometrically on the
left on
X
, and
\theta
acts as an isometry of

X
.

By
[
Kn86
, Proposition 1.2]
, any element
\gamma\in G
factorizes uniquely in the form

(2.6)

\displaystyle\gamma=e^{a}k^{-1},

\displaystyle a\in\mathfrak{p},\qquad k\in K.

If
\gamma,g\in G
, set

(2.7)

C\left(g\right)\gamma=g\gamma g^{-1}.

Then
C\left(g\right)
is an automorphism of
G
. Its derivative at
the identity is
the adjoint representation
g\in G\to\mathrm{Ad}\left(g\right)\in\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
. The derivative of this last map is
given by
a\in\mathfrak{g}\to\mathrm{ad}\left(a\right)\in\mathrm{End}\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
, with

\mathrm{ad}\left(a\right)b=\left[a,b\right]
. If
\gamma\in G
, the fixed point set
of
C\left(\gamma\right)
is the centralizer

Z\left(\gamma\right)\subset G
, whose Lie algebra
\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)
is
given by

(2.8)

\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)=\ker\left(1-\mathrm{Ad}\left(\gamma\right)\right).

If
f\in\mathfrak{g}
, let
Z\left(f\right)\subset G
be the
stabilizer of
f
. Its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{z}\left(f\right)\subset\mathfrak{g}
is given by

(2.9)

\mathfrak{z}\left(f\right)=\ker\mathrm{ad}\left(f\right).

In the sequel, if
M
is a Lie group, we denote by

M^{0}
the
connected component of the identity.

2.2.
Semisimple elements and their displacement function

Let
d
be the Riemannian distance on
X
. By
[
BaGS85
, § 6.1]
,
d

is a convex function on
X\times X
. If
\gamma\in G
, let

d_{\gamma}
be the corresponding displacement function on
X
, i.e.,

(2.10)

d_{\gamma}\left(x\right)=d\left(x,\gamma x\right).

If
g\in G
, then

(2.11)

d_{C\left(g\right)\gamma}\left(gx\right)=d_{\gamma}\left(x\right).

Moreover,

(2.12)

d_{\theta\left(\gamma\right)}\left(\theta x\right)=d_{\gamma}\left(x\right).

Set

(2.13)

m_{\gamma}=\inf d_{\gamma}.

Let
X\left(\gamma\right)\subset X
be the closed subset where

d_{\gamma}
reaches its minimum. By
[
BaGS85
, p. 78 and §1.2]
,
X\left(\gamma\right)
is a
closed convex subset,
d_{\gamma}
is smooth on
X\setminus X\left(\gamma\right)
and has no critical
points on
X\setminus X\left(\gamma\right)
.
Also by (
2.11
), (
2.12
),

(2.14)

\displaystyle X\left(C\left(g\right)\gamma\right)=gX\left(\gamma\right),

\displaystyle X\left(\theta\gamma\right)=\theta X\left(\gamma\right),

\displaystyle m_{C\left(g\right)\gamma}=m_{\gamma},

\displaystyle m_{\theta\left(\gamma\right)}=m_{\gamma}.

By
[
E96
, Definition 2.19.21]
,
\gamma
is said to be semisimple if
X\left(\gamma\right)
is
nonempty. If
\gamma
is semisimple, then
C\left(g\right)\gamma
and

\theta\left(\gamma\right)
are semisimple. Also
\gamma
is said to
be elliptic if it is semisimple and
m_{\gamma}=0
. Elliptic elements
are exactly the group elements that are conjugate to elements of
K
.
Finally,
\gamma
is said to be hyperbolic if it is conjugate to

e^{a},a\in\mathfrak{p}
.

By
[
Ko73
, Proposition 2.1]
,
[
BaGS85
, Theorems 2.19.23 and 2.19.24]
,
\gamma\in G
is semisimple if and only if it factorizes as

\gamma=he=eh
, with commuting hyperbolic
h
and elliptic
e
.
Also

e,h
are uniquely determined by
\gamma
, and

(2.15)

Z\left(\gamma\right)=Z\left(h\right)\cap Z\left(e\right).

Set

(2.16)

x_{0}=p1.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
\gamma\in G
be semisimple. If
g\in G,x=pg\in X
, then

x\in X\left(\gamma\right)
if and only if there exist
a\in\mathfrak{p},k\in K
such that
\mathrm{Ad}\left(k\right)a=a
, and

(2.17)

\gamma=C\left(g\right)\left(e^{a}k^{-1}\right).

Also
C\left(g\right)e^{a}\in G,C\left(g\right)k\in G
are uniquely
determined by
\gamma
. If
g_{t}=ge^{ta}
, then
t\in[0,1]\to y_{t}=pg_{t}

is the unique geodesic connecting
x
and
\gamma x
. Moreover

(2.18)

m_{\gamma}=\left|a\right|.

If
\gamma\in G
is semisimple, then
x_{0}\in X\left(\gamma\right)
if and only if there exist
a\in\mathfrak{p},k\in K
such that

(2.19)

\displaystyle\gamma=e^{a}k^{-1},

\displaystyle a\in\mathfrak{p},\qquad\mathrm{Ad}\left(k\right)a=a.

Also
a,k
are uniquely determined by (
2.19
).

Proof.

The first part of our theorem was established in
[
B11
, Theorem
3.1.2]
.
By taking
g=1
in the first part, we obtain the second part.
∎

Let
\gamma\in G
be a semisimple element written as in (
2.19
).
By
[
B11
, Proposition 3.2.8 and eqs. (3.3.4), (3.3.6)]
,

(2.20)

\displaystyle Z\left(e^{a}\right)=Z\left(a\right),

\displaystyle Z\left(\gamma\right)=Z\left(a\right)\cap Z\left(k\right),\qquad\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)=\mathfrak{z}\left(a\right)\cap\mathfrak{z}\left(k\right).

By (
2.19
),
a\in\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)
, and by
(
2.20
),
\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)\subset\mathfrak{z}\left(a\right)
, so that
a
is an element of the center of

\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)
.

Clearly,

(2.21)

\theta\left(\gamma\right)=e^{-a}k^{-1}.

Therefore
\theta\left(\gamma\right)\in Z\left(\gamma\right)
, so
that the above centralizers and Lie algebras are preserved by

\theta
. Set

(2.22)

K\left(\gamma\right)=Z\left(\gamma\right)\cap K.

By
[
B11
, Theorem 3.3.1]
, we have the identity

(2.23)

K^{0}\left(\gamma\right)=Z^{0}\left(\gamma\right)\cap K,

and
K^{0}\left(\gamma\right)
is a maximal compact subgroup of

Z^{0}\left(\gamma\right)
.

Put

(2.24)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}\left(\gamma\right)=\mathfrak{p}\cap\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)=\mathfrak{k}\cap\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right).

Then

\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
is the Lie algebra of

K\left(\gamma\right)
. We use similar notation for the Lie algebras

\mathfrak{z}\left(k\right),\mathfrak{z}\left(a\right)
.
We have the Cartan decompositions of Lie algebras,

(2.25)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)=\mathfrak{p}\left(\gamma\right)\oplus\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{z}\left(k\right)=\mathfrak{p}\left(k\right)\oplus\mathfrak{k}\left(k\right),\qquad

\displaystyle\mathfrak{z}\left(a\right)=\mathfrak{p}\left(a\right)\oplus\mathfrak{k}\left(a\right).

Then
B
restricts to a nondegenerate form on

\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right),\mathfrak{z}\left(k\right),\mathfrak{z}\left(a\right)
, so that

Z\left(\gamma\right),Z\left(k\right),Z\left(a\right)
are
possibly nonconnected reductive subgroups of
G
. By
[
B11
, Theorem
3.3.1]
, we have the identification of finite groups,

(2.26)

Z^{0}\left(\gamma\right)\setminus Z\left(\gamma\right)=K^{0}\left(\gamma\right)\setminus K\left(\gamma\right).

Let

\mathfrak{z}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right),\mathfrak{z}^{\perp}\left(a\right)
be the orthogonal spaces to

\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right),\mathfrak{z}\left(a\right)
in

\mathfrak{g}
with
respect to
B
. We have splittings

(2.27)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{z}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right)=\mathfrak{p}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right)\oplus\mathfrak{k}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{z}^{\perp}\left(a\right)=\mathfrak{p}^{\perp}\left(a\right)\oplus\mathfrak{k}^{\perp}\left(a\right).

Let

\mathfrak{z}^{\perp}_{a}\left(\gamma\right)
denote the
orthogonal to
\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)
in
\mathfrak{z}\left(a\right)
. We still have a splitting

(2.28)

\mathfrak{z}^{\perp}_{a}\left(\gamma\right)=\mathfrak{p}^{\perp}_{a}\left(\gamma\right)\oplus\mathfrak{k}^{\perp}_{a}\left(\gamma\right).

Now we recall a result established in
[
B11
, Theorem 3.3.1]
.

Theorem 2.2
.

The set
X\left(\gamma\right)
is preserved by
\theta
. Moreover,

(2.29)

X\left(\gamma\right)=X\left(e^{a}\right)\cap X\left(k\right).

Also
X\left(\gamma\right)
is a totally geodesic submanifold of
X
. In the
geodesic coordinate system centered at
x_{0}=p1
, then

(2.30)

X\left(\gamma\right)=\mathfrak{p}\left(\gamma\right).

The actions of
Z^{0}\left(\gamma\right),Z\left(\gamma\right)
on
X\left(\gamma\right)

are transitive. More precisely the maps
g\in Z^{0}\left(\gamma\right)\to pg\in X,g\in Z\left(\gamma\right)\to pg\in X
induce the identification of

Z^{0}\left(\gamma\right)
-manifolds,

(2.31)

X\left(\gamma\right)=Z^{0}\left(\gamma\right)/K^{0}\left(\gamma\right)=Z\left(\gamma\right)/K\left(\gamma\right).

Now we will establish a simple important consequence of Theorem

2.2
.

Theorem 2.3
.

Let
\gamma
be a semisimple element of
G
as in
(
2.19
). Let
\gamma^{\prime}
be another semisimple element of
G
such that

(2.32)

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}=e^{a^{\prime}}k^{\prime-1},

\displaystyle a^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{p},\qquad\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{\prime}\right)a^{\prime}=a^{\prime}.

Then there exists
g\in G
such that
\gamma^{\prime}=C\left(g\right)\gamma

if and only if there exists
k^{\prime\prime}\in K
such that

C\left(k^{\prime\prime}\right)\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}
, in which case

(2.33)

\displaystyle a^{\prime}=\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{\prime\prime}\right)a,

\displaystyle k^{\prime}=C\left(k^{\prime\prime}\right)k.

Proof.

Assume that
\gamma^{\prime}=C\left(g\right)\gamma
. By (
2.14
), we
get

(2.34)

X\left(\gamma^{\prime}\right)=gX\left(\gamma\right).

By Theorem
2.1
,
x_{0}\in X\left(\gamma\right)\cap X\left(\gamma^{\prime}\right)
. By (
2.34
),

gx_{0}\in X\left(\gamma^{\prime}\right)
. By Theorem
2.2
, there
exists
h\in Z\left(\gamma^{\prime}\right)
such that

(2.35)

hgx_{0}=x_{0},

which is equivalent to

(2.36)

k^{\prime\prime}=hg\in K.

Since
h\in Z\left(\gamma^{\prime}\right)
, we conclude that

C\left(k^{\prime\prime}\right)\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}
. Using the uniqueness of
decomposition in (
2.32
), equation (
2.33
) follows. The proof
of our theorem is completed.
∎

2.3.
Enveloping algebra and the Casimir

We identify
\mathfrak{g}
with the Lie algebra of left-invariant
vector fields on
G
. Let

U\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
be the enveloping algebra of

\mathfrak{g}
. Then
U\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)

can be identified with the algebra of left-invariant differential
operators on
G
. Let
{\@index{\@indexphrase[Zg]{$Z\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)$}}}Z\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)\subset U\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
denote the center of
U\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
.

If
E
is a finite-dimensional real or complex
vector space, and if
\rho^{E}:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathrm{End}\left(E\right)
is
a morphism of Lie algebras, the map
\rho^{E}
extends to a morphism

U\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)\to\mathrm{End}\left(E\right)
.

Among the elements of
Z\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
, there is the Casimir element

C^{\mathfrak{g}}
. If
e_{1},\ldots,e_{m+n}
is a basis of
\mathfrak{g}
, and if

e^{*}_{1},\ldots,e_{m+n}^{*}
is the dual basis of
\mathfrak{g}

with respect to
B
, then
4
4

4

In
[
Kn86
, Section 8.3]
, the
Casimir is defined with the opposite sign. We have adopted the sign
conventions of
[
B11
]
, which are closer to analysis.

(2.37)

C^{\mathfrak{g}}=-\sum_{i=1}^{m+n}e_{i}^{*}e_{i}.

If we consider instead the Lie algebra
\left(\mathfrak{k},B|_{\mathfrak{k}}\right)
,

C^{\mathfrak{k}}\in Z\left(\mathfrak{k}\right)
denotes the associated
Casimir element.

If
e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}
is a basis of
\mathfrak{p}
, and if

e^{*}_{1},\ldots,e^{*}_{m}
is the dual basis of
\mathfrak{p}

with respect to
B|_{\mathfrak{p}}
, set

(2.38)

C^{\mathfrak{p}}=-\sum_{i=1}^{m}e^{*}_{i}e_{i}.

Then
C^{\mathfrak{p}}\in U\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
.
Using (
2.37
), (
2.38
), we get

(2.39)

C^{\mathfrak{g}}=C^{\mathfrak{p}}+C^{\mathfrak{k}}.

Also
C^{\mathfrak{p}}
and
C^{\mathfrak{k}}
commute.

If
\rho^{E}:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathrm{End}\left(E\right)
is taken as above,
put

(2.40)

C^{\mathfrak{g},E}=\rho^{E}\left(C^{\mathfrak{g}}\right).

Under the above conditions, we can define
C^{\mathfrak{p},E},C^{\mathfrak{k},E}
.

Since
\mathfrak{g}
is itself a representation of
\mathfrak{g}
,

C^{\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}}
is the action of
C^{\mathfrak{g}}
on
\mathfrak{g}
.
Since
\mathfrak{k}

acts on
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{k}
,
C^{\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{p}},C^{\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{k}}
are also well-defined.

Proposition 2.4
.

The following identity holds:

(2.41)

\mathrm{Tr}\left[C^{\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}}\right]=3\mathrm{Tr}\left[C^{\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{p}}\right]+\mathrm{Tr}\left[C^{\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{k}}\right].

Proof.

By (
2.39
), we get

(2.42)

\mathrm{Tr}\left[C^{\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}}\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[C^{\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{g}}\right]+\mathrm{Tr}\left[C^{\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{g}}\right].

Let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathfrak{p}
,
and let
e_{m+1},\ldots,e_{n}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathfrak{k}
. Then

(2.43)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[C^{\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{g}}\right]=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq m+n\end{subarray}}\left|\left[e_{i},e_{j}\right]\right|^{2},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[C^{\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{p}}\right]=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
m+1\leq j\leq m+n\end{subarray}}\left|\left[e_{i},e_{j}\right]\right|^{2}=-\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq m}\left|\left[e_{i},e_{j}\right]\right|^{2}.

By (
2.43
), we deduce that

(2.44)

\mathrm{Tr}\left[C^{\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{g}}\right]=2\mathrm{Tr}\left[C^{\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{p}}\right].

Also

(2.45)

\mathrm{Tr}\left[C^{\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{g}}\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[C^{\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{p}}\right]+\mathrm{Tr}\left[C^{\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{k}}\right].

By (
2.42
), (
2.44
), and (
2.45
), we get (
2.41
). The proof of our proposition is completed.
∎

Let
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
be a
\theta
-stable Cartan subalgebra.
Then we have the splitting
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{p}}\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
.

5
5

5

More details will be given in Section
3
on Cartan subalgebras
and root systems.
Let
R\subset\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
be the corresponding root system.
Let
R_{+}
be a positive
root system. If
R_{-}=-R_{+}
,
R
is the disjoint union of

R_{+}
and
R_{-}
.
Let

\rho^{\mathfrak{g}}\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}
be the half sum of the positive roots.
Here
\rho^{\mathfrak{g}}\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}\oplus i\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{*}
. By Kostant’s strange formula
[
Ko76
]
, we have the identity

(2.46)

B^{*}\left(\rho^{\mathfrak{g}},\rho^{\mathfrak{g}}\right)=-\frac{1}{24}\mathrm{Tr}\left[C^{\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}}\right].

By (
2.41
), (
2.46
), we get

(2.47)

B^{*}\left(\rho^{\mathfrak{g}},\rho^{\mathfrak{g}}\right)=-\frac{1}{8}\mathrm{Tr}\left[C^{\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{p}}\right]-\frac{1}{24}\mathrm{Tr}\left[C^{\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{k}}\right].

Using the notation in
[
B11
, eq. (2.6.11)]

6
6

6

The
definition of
\kappa^{\mathfrak{g}}
is not needed. The formula is
given for later reference.
, by
(
2.47
), we obtain

(2.48)

B^{*}\left(\rho^{\mathfrak{g}},\rho^{\mathfrak{g}}\right)=-\frac{1}{4}B^{*}\left(\kappa^{\mathfrak{g}},\kappa^{\mathfrak{g}}\right).

2.4.
The elliptic operator
C^{\mathfrak{g},X}

Let
E
be a finite-dimensional Hermitian vector space, and let

\rho^{E}:K\to U\left(E\right)
denote a unitary representation of
K
.
The Casimir
C^{\mathfrak{k},E}
is a self-adjoint nonpositive
endomorphism of
E
. If
\rho^{E}
is irreducible, then
C^{\mathfrak{k},E}

is a scalar.

Let
F
be the vector bundle on
X
,

(2.49)

F=G\times_{K}E.

Then
F
is a Hermitian vector bundle on
X
, which is equipped
with a canonical connection
\nabla^{F}
. Also
C^{\mathfrak{k},E}

descend to a parallel section
C^{\mathfrak{k},F}
of

\mathrm{End}\left(F\right)
. Moreover,
G
acts on
C^{\infty}\left(X,F\right)
, so that if
g\in G,s\in C^{\infty}\left(X,F\right)
, if
g_{*}
denotes the lift of the action of
g

to
F
,

(2.50)

gs\left(x\right)=g_{*}s\left(g^{-1}x\right).

The Casimir operator
C^{\mathfrak{g}}
descends
to a second order elliptic operator

C^{\mathfrak{g},X}
acting on
C^{\infty}\left(X,F\right)
, which commutes with
G
. Let

\Delta^{X}
denote the classical Bochner Laplacian acting on

C^{\infty}\left(X,F\right)
. By
[
B11
, eq. (2.13.2)]
,
the splitting (
2.39
) of
C^{\mathfrak{g}}
descends to the
splitting of
C^{\mathfrak{g},X}
,

(2.51)

C^{\mathfrak{g},X}=-\Delta^{X}+C^{\mathfrak{k},F}.

2.5.
Orbital integrals and the
Casimir

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{even}}\left(\mathbf{R}\right)
, and let
\widehat{\mu}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{even}}\left(\mathbf{R}\right)
be
its Fourier transform, i.e.,

(2.52)

\widehat{\mu}\left(y\right)=\int_{\mathbf{R}}e^{-2i\pi yx}\mu\left(x\right)dx.

We assume that there exists
C>0
such that for any
k\in\mathbf{N}
,
there is
c_{k}>0
such that

(2.53)

\left|\widehat{\mu}^{(k)}\left(y\right)\right|\leq c_{k}\exp\left(-Cy^{2}\right).

The above condition is verified if
\widehat{\mu}
has compact
support. For
t>0
, it is also verified by the Gaussian function

e^{-tx^{2}}
.

If
A\in\mathbf{R}
, the operator
\mu\left(\sqrt{C^{\mathfrak{g},X}+A}\right)
is self-adjoint
with a smooth kernel

\mu\left(\sqrt{C^{\mathfrak{g},X}+A}\right)\left(x,x^{\prime}\right)
with respect to the Riemannian
volume
dx^{\prime}
on
X
. As explained in

[
B11
, Section 6.2]
, using finite propagation speed for the
wave equation, condition (
2.53
) implies
that there exist
C>0,c>0
such that if
x,x^{\prime}\in X
, then

(2.54)

\left|\mu\left(\sqrt{C^{\mathfrak{g},X}+A}\right)\left(x,x^{\prime}\right)\right|\leq Ce^{-cd^{2}\left(x,x^{\prime}\right)}.

If
\widehat{\mu}
has
compact support, then
\mu\left(\sqrt{C^{\mathfrak{g},X}+A}\right)\left(x,x^{\prime}\right)
vanishes when
d\left(x,x^{\prime}\right)

is large enough.

As explained in
[
B11
, Section 6.2]
, the above condition
guarantees that if
\gamma\in G
is semisimple, the orbital integral

\mathrm{Tr}^{\left[\gamma\right]}\left[\mu\left(\sqrt{C^{\mathfrak{g},X}+A}\right)\right]
is well-defined.
Let us give more details
on our conventions.

Let
\gamma\in G
be taken as in (
2.19
). Let

N_{X\left(\gamma\right)/X}
be the orthogonal bundle to

TX\left(\gamma\right)
in
TX
. By
[
B11
, eq. (3.4.1)]
, we
have the identity

(2.55)

N_{X\left(\gamma\right)/X}=Z^{0}\left(\gamma\right)\times_{K^{0}\left(\gamma\right)}\mathfrak{p}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right).

Let
\mathcal{N}_{X\left(\gamma\right)/X}
be the total space of

N_{X\left(\gamma\right)/X}
. By
[
B11
, Theorem 3.4.1]
, the
normal geodesic coordinate system based at
X\left(\gamma\right)

gives a smooth identification of

\mathcal{N}_{X\left(\gamma\right)/X}
with
X
. Let
dx,dy,df
be the
Riemannian volume forms on
X,X\left(\gamma\right),N_{X\left(\gamma\right)/X}
. Then
dydf
is a volume form on

\mathcal{N}_{X\left(\gamma\right)/X}
. Let
r\left(f\right)
denote
the corresponding Jacobian, so that we have the identity of volume
forms on
X
,

(2.56)

dx=r\left(f\right)dydf.

By
[
B11
, eq. (3.4.36)]
, there are constants
C>0,C^{\prime}>0
such that

(2.57)

r\left(f\right)\leq Ce^{C^{\prime}\left|f\right|}.

By
[
B11
, Theorem 3.4.1]
, there exists
C_{\gamma}>0
such
that for
f\in\mathfrak{p}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right),\left|f\right|\geq 1
,

(2.58)

d_{\gamma}\left(e^{f}x_{0}\right)\geq\left|a\right|+C_{\gamma}\left|f\right|.

As explained in
[
B11
, eq. (4.2.6)]
, by
(
2.54
), (
2.58
), there exist
C_{\gamma}>0,c_{\gamma}>0
such that if
f\in\mathfrak{p}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right)
, then

(2.59)

\left|\mu\left(\sqrt{C^{\mathfrak{g},X}+A}\right)\left(\gamma^{-1}e^{f}x_{0},e^{f}x_{0}\right)\right|\leq C_{\gamma}\exp\left(-c_{\gamma}\left|f\right|^{2}\right).

We denote by

\gamma_{*}
the action of
\gamma
on
F
. More
precisely, if
x\in X
,
\gamma_{*}
maps
F_{x}
into
F_{\gamma x}
.

In
[
B11
, Definition 4.2.2]
, the orbital integral

\mathrm{Tr}^{\left[\gamma\right]}\left[\mu\left(\sqrt{C^{\mathfrak{g},X}+A}\right)\right]

is defined
by the formula

(2.60)

\mathrm{Tr}^{\left[\gamma\right]}\left[\mu\left(\sqrt{C^{\mathfrak{g},X}+A}\right)\right]\\
=\int_{\mathfrak{p}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right)}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\gamma_{*}\mu\left(\sqrt{C^{\mathfrak{g},X}+A}\right)\left(\gamma^{-1}e^{f}x_{0},e^{f}x_{0}\right)\right]r\left(f\right)df.

Equations (
2.57
), (
2.59
) guarantee that the
integral in (
2.60
) converges.

Let
dk
be the Haar measure on
K
such that
\mathrm{Vol}\left(K\right)=1
.
Then
dg=dxdk
is a Haar measure on
G
. Let
dy
be the Riemannian
volume form on
X\left(\gamma\right)
. Let
dk^{\prime 0}
be the
Haar measure on
K^{0}\left(\gamma\right)
such that

\mathrm{Vol}\left(K^{0}\left(\gamma\right)\right)=1
. Then

dz^{0}=dydk^{\prime 0}
is a Haar measure on

Z^{0}\left(\gamma\right)
. Let
dv^{0}
be the volume on

Z^{0}\left(\gamma\right)\setminus G
such that
dg=dz^{0}dv^{0}
.

As explained in
[
B11
, Section 4.2]
, the smooth kernel

\mu\left(\sqrt{C^{\mathfrak{g},X}+A}\right)\left(x,x^{\prime}\right)

lifts to a smooth
function on
G
with values in
\mathrm{End}\left(E\right)
, denoted

\mu^{E}\left(\sqrt{C^{\mathfrak{g},X}+A}\right)\left(g\right),

and by
[
B11
, eq. (4.2.11)]
, we have the identity

(2.61)

\mathrm{Tr}^{\left[\gamma\right]}\left[\mu\left(\sqrt{C^{\mathfrak{g},X}+A}\right)\right]\\
=\int_{Z^{0}\left(\gamma\right)\setminus G}\mathrm{Tr}^{E}\left[\mu\left(\sqrt{C^{\mathfrak{g},X}+A}\right)\left(\left(v^{0}\right)^{-1}\gamma v^{0}\right)\right]dv^{0}.

This definition of orbital integrals coincides with the definition
given by Selberg
[
Se56
, p. 66]
.

2.6.
The function
\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}

We use the assumptions in Subsection
2.2
and the
corresponding notation. In particular
\gamma\in G
is a semisimple
element as in (
2.19
).

Then
\mathrm{Ad}\left(\gamma\right)
preserves
\mathfrak{z}\left(a\right),\mathfrak{z}^{\perp}\left(a\right)
. Also
\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{-1}\right)
preserves

\mathfrak{z}_{a}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right)
. If
Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\in\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
,
\mathrm{ad}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)

preserves
\mathfrak{z}_{a}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right)
. The splitting
(
2.28
) is preserved by
\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{-1}\right)
and

\mathrm{ad}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)
.

If
x\in\mathbf{R}
, put

(2.62)

\widehat{A}\left(x\right)=\frac{x/2}{\sinh\left(x/2\right)}.

If
Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\in\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
,
\mathrm{ad}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)

acts as an antisymmetric endomorphism of
\mathfrak{p}\left(\gamma\right),\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
, so that its
eigenvalues are either
0
, or they come by nonzero conjugate
imaginary pairs.
If
Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\in i\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
, put
7
7

7

This fits
with the classical notation in the theory of characteristic classes.

(2.63)

\displaystyle\widehat{A}\left(\mathrm{ad}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)|_{\mathfrak{p}\left(\gamma\right)}\right)=\left[\det\left(\widehat{A}\left(\mathrm{ad}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)|_{\mathfrak{p}\left(\gamma\right)}\right)\right)\right]^{1/2},

\displaystyle\widehat{A}\left(\mathrm{ad}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)|_{\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)}\right)=\left[\det\left(\widehat{A}\left(\mathrm{ad}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)|_{\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)}\right)\right)\right]^{1/2}.

The square root in (
2.63
) is the positive square
root of a positive real number.

We follow
[
B11
, Theorem 5.5.1]
, while slightly changing
the notation.

Definition 2.5
.

If
Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\in i\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
, put

(2.64)

\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)=\frac{\det\left(1-\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{-1}e^{-Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}\right)\right)|_{\mathfrak{k}^{\perp}_{a}\left(\gamma\right)}}{\det\left(1-\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{-1}e^{-Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}\right)\right)|_{\mathfrak{p}_{a}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right)}}.

Set

(2.65)

\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)=\left[\frac{1}{\det\left(1-\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{-1}\right)\right)|_{\mathfrak{z}^{\perp}_{a}\left(\gamma\right)}}\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)\right]^{1/2}.

The fact that the square root in (
2.65
) is unambiguously
defined is established in
[
B11
, Section 5.5]
. Let us
explain the details. First we make
Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}=0
. Then

(2.66)

\frac{1}{\det\left(1-\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{-1}\right)\right)|_{\mathfrak{z}^{\perp}_{a}\left(\gamma\right)}}\frac{\det\left(1-\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{-1}\right)\right)|_{\mathfrak{k}^{\perp}_{a}\left(\gamma\right)}}{\det\left(1-\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{-1}\right)\right)|_{\mathfrak{p}_{a}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right)}}\\
=\left[\frac{1}{\det\left(1-\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{-1}\right)\right)|_{\mathfrak{p}_{a}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right)}}\right]^{2}.

The conventions in
[
B11
]
say that the square root of
(
2.66
) is the obvious positive square root, i.e.,

(2.67)

\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}\left(0\right)=\frac{1}{\det\left(1-\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{-1}\right)\right)|_{\mathfrak{p}_{a}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right)}}.

Using analyticity in the variable
Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\in i\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
, the choice of the square root in
(
2.65
) determines a choice of the square root in
(
2.68
). This point
will be discussed at length in Section
4
. No choice of
a Cartan subalgebra or of a positive root system is
needed at this stage.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)
be the smooth function of

Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\in i\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
,

(2.68)

\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)=\frac{1}{\left|\det\left(1-\mathrm{Ad}\left(\gamma\right)\right)|_{\mathfrak{z}^{\perp}\left(a\right)}\right|^{1/2}}\frac{\widehat{A}\left(\mathrm{ad}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)|_{\mathfrak{p}\left(\gamma\right)}\right)}{\widehat{A}\left(\mathrm{ad}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)|_{\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)}\right)}\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right).

With the conventions in
[
B11
, Chapter 5]
, where instead a
function
J_{\gamma}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)
is defined on
\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
, we have

(2.69)

J_{\gamma}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)=\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}\left(iY_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right).

By
[
B11
, eq. (5.5.11)]
or by (
2.68
), if
Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\in i\mathfrak{k}
, then

(2.70)

\mathcal{J}_{1}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)=\frac{\widehat{A}\left(\mathrm{ad}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)|_{\mathfrak{p}}\right)}{\widehat{A}\left(\mathrm{ad}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)|_{\mathfrak{k}}\right)}.

2.7.
Some properties of the function
\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}

Let
\rho^{E^{*}}:K\to U\left(E^{*}\right)
denote the representation of
K
which is dual to
the representation
\rho^{E}
.

Proposition 2.7
.

If
Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\in i\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
, then

(2.71)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\gamma^{-1}}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)=\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}\left(-Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}^{E^{*}}\left[\rho^{E^{*}}\left(ke^{-Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}\right)\right]=\mathrm{Tr}^{E}\left[\rho^{E}\left(k^{-1}e^{Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}\right)\right],

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)}=\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}\left(-Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right),

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{Tr}^{E}\left[\rho^{E}\left(k^{-1}e^{-Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}\right)\right]}=\mathrm{Tr}^{E^{*}}\left[\rho^{E^{*}}\left(k^{-1}e^{Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}\right)\right].

Proof.

If
f\in\mathrm{End}\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
, let
\widetilde{f}\in\mathrm{End}\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
denote
the adjoint of
f
with respect to
B
. We have the identity

(2.72)

\mathrm{Ad}\left(\gamma^{-1}\right)=\widetilde{\mathrm{Ad}\left(\gamma\right)}.

By (
2.72
), we deduce that

(2.73)

\det\left(1-\mathrm{Ad}\left(\gamma^{-1}\right)\right)|_{\mathfrak{z}^{\perp}\left(a\right)}=\det\left(1-\mathrm{Ad}\left(\gamma\right)\right)|_{\mathfrak{z}^{\perp}\left(a\right)}.

A similar argument shows that

(2.74)

\displaystyle\det\left(1-\mathrm{Ad}\left(ke^{-Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}\right)\right)|_{\mathfrak{p}_{a}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right)}=\det\left(1-\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{-1}e^{Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}\right)\right)|_{\mathfrak{p}_{a}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right)},

\displaystyle\det\left(1-\mathrm{Ad}\left(ke^{-Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}\right)\right)|_{\mathfrak{k}_{a}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right)}=\det\left(1-\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{-1}e^{Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}\right)\right)|_{\mathfrak{k}_{a}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right)}.

By (
2.64
), (
2.65
), (
2.68
),
(
2.73
), and
(
2.74
), we get the first identity in (
2.71
). The
second identity in (
2.71
) is trivial.

If
Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\in i\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
,

(2.75)

\displaystyle\overline{\det\left(1-\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{-1}e^{-Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}\right)\right)|_{\mathfrak{p}_{a}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right)}}=\det\left(1-\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{-1}e^{Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}\right)\right)|_{\mathfrak{p}_{a}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right)},

\displaystyle\overline{\det\left(1-\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{-1}e^{-Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}\right)\right)|_{\mathfrak{k}_{a}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right)}}=\det\left(1-\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{-1}e^{Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}\right)\right)|_{\mathfrak{k}_{a}^{\perp}\left(\gamma\right)}.

By (
2.75
), we get the third identity in (
2.71
).
Since
Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\in i\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
, the fourth identity is
trivial.
The proof of our proposition is completed.
∎

2.8.
A geometric formula for the orbital integrals associated
with the Casimir

Note that
i\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
is naturally an Euclidean
vector space. If
Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\in i\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
, we denote by

\left|Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right|
its Euclidean norm. More precisely, if

Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\in i\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
, then

(2.76)

\left|Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right|^{2}=B\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}},Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right).

By

[
B11
, eq. (6.1.1)]
, there exist

c>0,C>0
such that if
Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\in i\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
,

(2.77)

\left|\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)\right|\leq c\exp\left(C\left|Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right|\right).

In the sequel,
\int_{i\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)}
denotes integration on the real vector space

i\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
.

Let
dY_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}
be the Euclidean volume on
i\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
. Set
p=\dim\mathfrak{p}\left(\gamma\right),q=\dim\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)
.
Now we state the result obtained in
[
B11
, Theorem
6.1.1]
. Our reformulation takes equation
(
2.48
) into account.

Theorem 2.8
.

For
t>0
, the following identity holds:

(2.78)

\mathrm{Tr}^{\left[\gamma\right]}\left[\exp\left(-tC^{\mathfrak{g},X}/2\right)\right]=\exp\left(-tB^{*}\left(\rho^{\mathfrak{g}},\rho^{\mathfrak{g}}\right)/2\right)\frac{\exp\left(-\left|a\right|^{2}/2t\right)}{\left(2\pi t\right)^{p/2}}\\
\int_{i\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)}\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)\mathrm{Tr}^{E}\left[\rho^{E}\left(k^{-1}e^{-Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}\right)\right]\exp\left(-\left|Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right|^{2}/2t\right)\frac{dY_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}{\left(2\pi t\right)^{q/2}}.

Let
B|_{\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)}
be the restriction of

B
to
\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)
, and let

B^{*}|_{\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)}
be the corresponding
quadratic form on
\mathfrak{z}^{*}\left(\gamma\right)
. Let
\Delta^{\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)}
denote the associated
generalized Laplacian on
\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)
. We can extend
\Delta^{\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)}
to an
operator acting via constant holomorphic vector fields on
\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)_{\mathbf{C}}
.

Put

(2.79)

\mathfrak{z}_{i}\left(\gamma\right)=\mathfrak{p}\left(\gamma\right)\oplus i\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right).

Then
B|_{\mathfrak{z}_{i}\left(\gamma\right)}
is a scalar
product on
\mathfrak{z}_{i}\left(\gamma\right)
.
The generalized Laplacian

\Delta^{\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)}
restricts on
\mathfrak{z}_{i}\left(\gamma\right)
to the
standard Euclidean Laplacian of
\mathfrak{z}_{i}\left(\gamma\right)
.

We take
\mu\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{even}}\left(\mathbf{R}\right)
as in
Subsection
2.5
.
If
f\in\mathfrak{z}_{i}\left(\gamma\right)
, let

\mu\left(\sqrt{-\Delta^{\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)}+B^{*}\left(\rho^{\mathfrak{g}},\rho^{\mathfrak{g}}\right)+A}\right)\left(f\right)

be the smooth convolution kernel for

\mu\left(\sqrt{-\Delta^{\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)}+B^{*}\left(\rho^{\mathfrak{g}},\rho^{\mathfrak{g}}\right)+A}\right)
on
\mathfrak{z}_{i}\left(\gamma\right)
with
respect to the volume form associated with the scalar product of
\mathfrak{z}_{i}\left(\gamma\right)
. Using (
2.53
) and finite
propagation speed for the wave equation, there exist
C>0,c>0
such
that if
f\in\mathfrak{z}_{i}\left(\gamma\right)
, then

(2.80)

\left|\mu\left(\sqrt{-\Delta^{\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)}+B^{*}\left(\rho^{\mathfrak{g}},\rho^{\mathfrak{g}}\right)+A}\right)\left(f\right)\right|\leq Ce^{-c\left|f\right|^{2}}.

Let
\delta_{a}
be the Dirac mass at
a\in\mathfrak{p}\left(\gamma\right)
. Then

\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)\mathrm{Tr}^{E}\left[\rho^{E}\left(k^{-1}e^{-Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}\right)\right]\delta_{a}

is a distribution on
\mathfrak{z}_{i}\left(\gamma\right)
, to which
the smooth convolution kernel

\mu\left(\sqrt{-\Delta^{\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)}+B^{*}\left(\rho^{\mathfrak{g}},\rho^{\mathfrak{g}}\right)+A}\right)

can be applied. By definition,

(2.81)

\mu\left(\sqrt{-\Delta^{\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)}+B^{*}\left(\rho^{\mathfrak{g}},\rho^{\mathfrak{g}}\right)+A}\right)\\
\left[\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)\mathrm{Tr}^{E}\left[\rho^{E}\left(k^{-1}e^{-Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}\right)\right]\delta_{a}\right]\left(0\right)\\
=\int_{i\mathfrak{k}\left(\gamma\right)}\mu\left(\sqrt{-\Delta^{\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)}+B^{*}\left(\rho^{\mathfrak{g}},\rho^{\mathfrak{g}}\right)+A}\right)\left(-Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}},-a\right)\\
\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)\mathrm{Tr}^{E}\left[\rho^{E}\left(k^{-1}e^{-Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}\right)\right]dY_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}.

In the right hand-side of (
2.81
),
\left(-Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}},-a\right)

can as well be replaced by
\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}},a\right)
.

In
[
B11
, Theorem 6.2.2]
, the following extension of
Theorem
2.8
was established.

Theorem 2.9
.

The following identity holds:

(2.82)

\mathrm{Tr}^{\left[\gamma\right]}\left[\mu\left(\sqrt{C^{\mathfrak{g},X}+A}\right)\right]=\mu\left(\sqrt{-\Delta^{\mathfrak{z}\left(\gamma\right)}+B^{*}\left(\rho^{\mathfrak{g}},\rho^{\mathfrak{g}}\right)+A}\right)\\
\left[\mathcal{J}_{\gamma}\left(Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}\right)\mathrm{Tr}^{E}\left[\rho^{E}\left(k^{-1}e^{-Y_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}}}\right)\right]\delta_{a}\right]\left(0\right).

3.
Cartan subalgebras, Cartan subgroups, and root systems

The purpose of this Section is to recall basic facts on Cartan
subalgebras, on Cartan subgroups, and on root systems.

This section is organized as follows. In Subsection

3.1
, we state some elementary facts of linear algebra.

In Subsection
3.2
, we recall the definition of Cartan
subalgebras.

In Subsection
3.3
, we introduce the corresponding
root system, and the associated algebraic Weyl group.

In Subsection
3.4
, we define the real and the imaginary
roots.

In Subsection
3.5
, we construct a positive root
system.

In Subsection
3.6
, when the Cartan subalgebra is
fundamental, we compare the root system of
\mathfrak{k}
with the
root system of
\mathfrak{g}
.

In Subsection
3.7
, we introduce the Cartan
subgroups, and of the corresponding regular elements.

In Subsection
3.8
, we relate
semisimple elements in
G
to Cartan subgroups.

In Subsection
3.9
, we describe the characters of
Cartan subgroups associated with a root system.

In Subsection
3.10
, we give some properties
of the real and imaginary roots with respect to semisimple elements
in
G
.

Finally, in Subsection
3.11
, we give a well-known formula that
relates the action of invariant differential operators on the Lie
algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and on a Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
.

We make the same assumptions as in Section
2
, and we
use the corresponding notation.

3.1.
Linear algebra

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional real vector space. The symmetric
algebras

S^{\cdot}\left(V\right),S^{\cdot}\left(V^{*}\right)
are the
algebras of polynomials on
V^{*},V
. If
v\in V
,
v
acts as a
derivation of
S^{\cdot}\left(V^{*}\right)
. More generally,

S^{\cdot}\left(V\right)
acts on
S^{\cdot}\left(V^{*}\right)
, and this
action identifies
S^{\cdot}\left(V\right)
with the algebra

D^{\cdot}\left(V\right)
of real partial differential operators on
V

with constant coefficients. In particular, if
B^{*}\in S^{2}\left(V\right)
is a bilinear symmetric form on
V^{*}
, the associated
element in
D^{\cdot}\left(V\right)
is the corresponding
Laplacian
\Delta^{V}
. If
B^{*}
is positive,
\Delta^{V}
is just a classical Laplacian.
If
B^{*}
is negative, then
\Delta^{V}
is the
negative of a
classical Laplacian on
V
.

Let
V_{\mathbf{C}}=V\otimes_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}
be the
complexification of
V
, a complex vector
space. Its complex dual is given by
V^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}=V^{*}\otimes_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}
.
The algebras
S^{\cdot}\left(V_{\mathbf{C}}\right),S^{\cdot}\left(V^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
are the
algebras of complex polynomials on
V^{*},V
. Note that

(3.1)

\displaystyle S^{\cdot}\left(V_{\mathbf{C}}\right)=S^{\cdot}\left(V\right)\otimes_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C},

\displaystyle S^{\cdot}\left(V^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)=S^{\cdot}\left(V^{*}\right)\otimes_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}.

Put

(3.2)

\displaystyle D^{\cdot}\left(V_{\mathbf{C}}\right)=D\left(V\right)\otimes_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C},

\displaystyle D^{\cdot}\left(V^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)=D^{\cdot}\left(V^{*}\right)\otimes_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}.

Then
D^{\cdot}\left(V_{\mathbf{C}}\right),D^{\cdot}\left(V^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
are the
complexifications of

D^{\cdot}\left(V\right),D^{\cdot}\left(V^{*}\right)
, and also the spaces
of complex holomorphic differential operators with constant
coefficients on
V_{\mathbf{C}},V^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}
.

In particular, if
B^{*}\in S^{2}\left(V\right)
,
\Delta^{V}
is now
viewed as a holomorphic operator on
V_{\mathbf{C}}
, that coincides with the
corresponding Laplacian
\Delta^{V}
on
V
, and with
-\Delta^{V}

on
iV\simeq V
.
8
8

8

On
\mathbf{C}\simeq\mathbf{R}^{2}
,
when acting on holomorphic functions, the differential operators

\frac{\partial}{\partial z},\frac{\partial}{\partial x},-i\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
coincide.
In this sense, the differential operator
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
on
\mathbf{R}

extends to the differential operator
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
on
\mathbf{C}
, and
restricts to the operator
-i\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
on the
imaginary line
i\mathbf{R}
. The operator
\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}
on

\mathbf{R}
restricts to the operator
-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y^{2}}
on
i\mathbf{R}
.

Also
S^{\cdot}\left(V^{*}\right)\subset C^{\infty}\left(V,\mathbf{R}\right)
, and the action of

D^{\cdot}\left(V\right)
extends to
C^{\infty}\left(V,\mathbf{R}\right)
.

Let

S\left[\left[V^{*}\right]\right]
be the algebra of
formal power series
s=\sum_{i=0}^{+\infty}s^{i},s^{i}\in S^{i}\left(V^{*}\right)
. Then
S\left[\left[V^{*}\right]\right]

can be identified with the algebra
D\left[\left[V^{*}\right]\right]

of differential operators of infinite order with constant
coefficients on
V^{*}
. In particular,

S\left[\left[V^{*}\right]\right]
acts on
S^{\cdot}\left(V\right)
.

3.2.
The Cartan subalgebras of
\mathfrak{g}

By
[
W88
, Section 0.2]
, a Lie subalgebra

\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
is
said to be a Cartan subalgebra if
\mathfrak{h}
is maximal among the
abelian subalgebras of
\mathfrak{g}
whose elements act as semisimple
endomorphisms of
\mathfrak{g}
. Cartan subalgebras are known
to exist and have the same dimension
r
, which is called the complex
rank of
G
. By
[
Kn02
, Proposition 6.64]
, there is a finite family
of nonconjugate Cartan subalgebras in
\mathfrak{g}
.
By
[
W88
, Lemma 2.3.3]
, in
every conjugacy class of Cartan subalgebras, there is a unique
\theta
-stable Cartan
algebra, up to conjugation by
K
. Therefore there is a finite family of nonconjugate

\theta
-stable Cartan subalgebras, up to conjugation by
K
. By

[
Kn02
, Theorem 2.15]
, the Cartan subalgebras of

\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{C}}
are unique up to automorphisms induced by the
adjoint group
\mathrm{Ad}\left(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
.

Let
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
be a
\theta
-stable Cartan
subalgebra. To the Cartan splitting of
\mathfrak{g}
in
(
2.1
) corresponds the splitting

(3.3)

\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{p}}\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}.

In particular the restriction
B|_{\mathfrak{h}}
of
B
to

\mathfrak{h}
is nondegenerate. This is also the case if
\mathfrak{h}
is any Cartan
subalgebra.

Up to conjugation by
K
, there is a unique
\theta
-stable Cartan
subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
, which is called fundamental, such that
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
is a
Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{k}
. Let us give more details on its
construction
[
Kn86
, pp. 129, 131]
.
Let

\mathfrak{t}\subset\mathfrak{k}
be a Cartan subalgebra of

\mathfrak{k}
. Let
\mathfrak{z}\left(\mathfrak{t}\right)\subset\mathfrak{g}
be the centralizer of
\mathfrak{t}
, i.e.,

(3.4)

\mathfrak{z}\left(\mathfrak{t}\right)=\left\{f\in\mathfrak{g},\left[\mathfrak{t},f\right]=0\right\}.

Then
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{z}\left(\mathfrak{t}\right)
is a

\theta
-stable
fundamental Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
, and
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}=\mathfrak{t}
.

An
element
f\in\mathfrak{g}
is said to be semisimple if

\mathrm{ad}\left(f\right)\in\mathrm{End}\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
is semisimple. If

\mathfrak{h}
is a Cartan subalgebra, elements of
\mathfrak{h}
are
semisimple. Any semisimple element of
\mathfrak{g}
lies in a Cartan
subalgebra.

If
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
is a Cartan subalgebra, let

\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}
be the orthogonal to
\mathfrak{h}
in

\mathfrak{g}
. We have the
B
-orthogonal splitting,

(3.5)

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h}^{\perp},

and
B
is also nondegenerate on
\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}
. If
\mathfrak{h}
is
\theta
-stable, then
\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}
is also

\theta
-stable, and so it splits as

(3.6)

\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}=\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}_{\mathfrak{p}}\oplus\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}_{\mathfrak{k}}.

Let

\mathfrak{u}=i\mathfrak{p}\oplus\mathfrak{k}
be the
compact form of
\mathfrak{g}
. Then

\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{u}}=i\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{p}}\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
is a Cartan
subalgebra of
\mathfrak{u}
. If
\mathfrak{h}
is
\theta
-stable,
then
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{u}}
is also
\theta
-stable.

An element
f\in\mathfrak{g}
is said to be regular if
\mathfrak{z}\left(f\right)
is a Cartan subalgebra. Regular elements in

\mathfrak{g}
are semisimple.

If
\mathfrak{h}
is a Cartan subalgebra, if
f\in\mathfrak{h}
,

\mathrm{ad}\left(f\right)
acts as an endomorphism of
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}
. Then
f\in\mathfrak{h}
is regular if and only if
\det\mathrm{ad}\left(f\right)|_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}\neq 0
.

3.3.
A root system and the Weyl group

Let
\mathfrak{h}
be a
\theta
-stable Cartan subalgebra.

Let

R\subset\mathfrak{h}^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}
be the root system
associated with
\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}

[
Kn02
, Section II.4]
. If
\alpha\in R
, then
-\alpha\in R,\overline{\alpha}\in R
. If

\alpha\in R
, let

\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{C}}

be the weight space associated with
\alpha
, which is of dimension

1
.
Then we have the splitting

(3.7)

\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{C}}=\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}\bigoplus\oplus_{\alpha\in R}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}.

If
\alpha\in R
, then

(3.8)

\mathfrak{g}_{\overline{\alpha}}=\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{\alpha}.

If
f\in\mathfrak{h}
,
\mathrm{ad}\left(f\right)\in\mathrm{End}\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)
is antisymmetric with respect to
B
, so that
the
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}|_{\alpha\in R}
are
B
-orthogonal to

\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}
.
If
\alpha,\beta\in R
, then
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{g}_{\beta}
are
B
-orthogonal except when
\beta=-\alpha
, and the
pairing between
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha}
is
nondegenerate, so that if
\alpha\in R
, the form
B
induces the
identification

(3.9)

\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha}\simeq\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}^{*}.

Also

(3.10)

\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}_{\mathbf{C}}=\oplus_{\alpha\in R}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}.

If

\alpha\in R
,
\alpha
takes real values on
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, and imaginary values on
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
, i.e.,
\alpha\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}_{\mathfrak{p}}\oplus i\mathfrak{h}^{*}_{\mathfrak{k}}
.
Also
\theta
preserves the splitting (
3.5
) of
\mathfrak{g}
, and it maps
R
into itself. More precisely, if
\alpha\in R
,

(3.11)

\displaystyle\theta\alpha=-\overline{\alpha},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{\theta\alpha}=\theta\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}.

Let

W\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}:\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)\subset\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
be the algebraic
Weyl group
[
Kn86
, p. 131]
. Then
R\subset i\mathfrak{h}^{*}_{\mathfrak{u}}
, and

W\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}:\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)\subset\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{u}}\right)
, i.e.,
W\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}:\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
preserves the real vector space

\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{u}}
. Also
\theta
acts as
an automorphism of the Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{u}
, and

W\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}:\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
is preserved by
conjugation by
\theta
.

In general,
W\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}:\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
does
not preserve the real vector space
\mathfrak{h}
. Recall that if

h\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}
, we can define its complex conjugate

\overline{h}\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}
. If
u\in\mathrm{End}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
, its complex conjugate

\overline{u}\in\mathrm{End}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
is such that if
h\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}
, then

(3.12)

\overline{u}\left(h\right)=\overline{u\left(\overline{h}\right)}.

Proposition 3.1
.

If
w\in W\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}:\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
, then

(3.13)

\overline{w}=\theta w\theta^{-1}.

In particular, the group
W\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}:\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
is
preserved by complex conjugation.

Proof.

The group
W\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}:\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
is
generated by the symmetries
s_{\alpha},\alpha\in R
with respect to the vanishing locus
of the
\alpha\in R
. By (
3.11
), we deduce that if

\alpha\in R
,

(3.14)

\overline{s_{\alpha}}=\theta s_{\alpha}\theta^{-1},

from which get (
3.13
). Since
W\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}:\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
is stable by conjugation by
\theta
, the
group
W\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}:\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
is preserved
by complex conjugation.

Another proof is as follows. Observe that there is a canonical
identification of complex vector spaces
\varphi:\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}\simeq\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{u},\mathbf{C}}
, but the complex conjugations on
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}
and on
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{u},\mathbf{C}}
are not the same. More precisely, if
h\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}
,

(3.15)

\overline{\varphi h}=\varphi\theta\overline{h}=\theta\varphi\overline{h}.

If
w\in W\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}:\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
, then

(3.16)

w|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}}=\varphi^{-1}w|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{u},\mathbf{C}}}\varphi.

Recall that
w
is a real automorphism of the real vector space

\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{u}}
. By (
3.15
), (
3.16
), we get

(3.17)

\overline{w}|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}}=\overline{\varphi}^{-1}w|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{u},\mathbf{C}}}\overline{\varphi}.

By (
3.15
)–(
3.17
), we get (
3.13
). The proof of our proposition is completed.
∎

3.4.
Real roots and imaginary roots

Let

R^{\mathrm{re}}\subset R
be the roots
\alpha\in R
such
that
\theta\alpha=-\alpha
, let

R^{\mathrm{im}}
be the roots
\alpha\in R
such that
\theta\alpha=\alpha
. These are
respectively the real roots and the imaginary roots. Imaginary roots
vanish on
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, real roots vanish on

\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
. By
[
Kn86
, p. 349]
, the set of complex roots

R^{\mathrm{c}}\subset R
is defined to be

(3.18)

R^{\mathrm{c}}=R\setminus\left(R^{\mathrm{re}}\cup R^{\mathrm{im}}\right).

Proposition 3.2
.

If
\alpha\in R
, the map
f\in\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{C}}\to\overline{f}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{C}}
induces an
antilinear isomorphism from
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
into
\mathfrak{g}_{-\theta\alpha}
, and the map
f\in\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{C}}\to\theta\overline{f}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbf{C}}
induces an antilinear
isomorphism from
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
into
\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha}
. If
\alpha\in R^{\mathrm{re}}
,
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
is the complexification of a real vector subspace of
\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}
.

Proof.

If
b\in\mathfrak{h},f\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
, then

(3.19)

\left[b,f\right]=\left\langle\alpha,b\right\rangle f.

By taking the conjugate of (
3.19
), we obtain

(3.20)

\left[b,\overline{f}\right]=\left\langle-\theta\alpha,b\right\rangle\overline{f}.

By (
3.20
), we get the first part of our proposition. By
composing this isomorphism with
\theta
, we obtain the second part.
If
\alpha\in R^{\mathrm{re}}
, then
-\theta\alpha=\alpha
, so that

\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{\alpha}}
is real.
The proof of our proposition is completed.
∎

Definition 3.3
.

Put

(3.21)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}=\ker\mathrm{ad}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{p}}\right)\cap\mathfrak{h}^{\perp},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}=\ker\mathrm{ad}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}\right)\cap\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}.

Then
\theta
acts on
\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{r}
, so that we have
the
splittings,

(3.22)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}=\mathfrak{i}_{\mathfrak{p}}\oplus\mathfrak{i}_{\mathfrak{k}},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}=\mathfrak{r}_{\mathfrak{p}}\oplus\mathfrak{r}_{\mathfrak{k}}.

Proposition 3.4
.

The vector spaces
\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{r}
are orthogonal in

\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}
. Moreover,

(3.23)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{\mathbf{C}}=\oplus_{\alpha\in R^{\mathrm{im}}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}_{\mathbf{C}}=\oplus_{\alpha\in R^{\mathrm{re}}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}.

If
\alpha\in R^{\mathrm{im}}
, then either
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\subset\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{C}}
, or
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\subset\mathfrak{k}_{\mathbf{C}}
.

Proof.

If
f\in\mathfrak{h}^{\perp},f\in\mathfrak{i}\cap\mathfrak{r}
, then
f
commutes with

\mathfrak{h}
. Since
\mathfrak{h}
is a Cartan subalgebra,

f=0
, so that
\mathfrak{i}\cap\mathfrak{r}=0
. If
\alpha\in R\setminus R^{\mathrm{im}}
,
\alpha
does not vanish identically on

\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, and its vanishing locus in

\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a
hyperplane. So one can find
b_{\mathfrak{p}}\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{p}}\setminus 0
such that for any
\alpha\in R\setminus R^{\mathrm{im}}
,

\left\langle\alpha,b_{\mathfrak{p}}\right\rangle\neq 0
. Then

(3.24)

\mathfrak{i}=\ker\mathrm{ad}\left(b_{\mathfrak{p}}\right)\cap\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}.

Since
\mathfrak{i}\cap\mathfrak{r}=0
,
\mathrm{ad}\left(b_{\mathfrak{p}}\right)
acts as an invertible morphism of
\mathfrak{r}
. Therefore
any element of
\mathfrak{r}
lies in the image of

\mathrm{ad}\left(b_{\mathfrak{p}}\right)
. Since
\mathrm{ad}\left(b_{\mathfrak{p}}\right)
is symmetric in the classical
sense,
\mathfrak{i}
and
\mathfrak{r}
are orthogonal. Equation (
3.23
) is elementary.
If
\alpha\in R^{\mathrm{im}}
, the action of
\mathfrak{h}
on

\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
factors through
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
. Also
\mathrm{ad}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}\right)

preserves the splitting
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{p}\oplus\mathfrak{k}
. Therefore if
\alpha\in R^{\mathrm{im}}
, either
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\subset\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{C}}
, or
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\subset\mathfrak{k}_{\mathbf{C}}
. The proof of our proposition is completed.
∎

Definition 3.5
.

Put

(3.25)

\displaystyle R^{\mathrm{im}}_{\mathfrak{p}}=\left\{\alpha\in R^{\mathrm{im}},\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\subset\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{C}}\right\},

\displaystyle R^{\mathrm{im}}_{\mathfrak{k}}=\left\{\alpha\in R^{\mathrm{im}},\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\subset\mathfrak{k}_{\mathbf{C}}\right\}.

Then

(3.26)

R^{\mathrm{im}}=R^{\mathrm{im}}_{\mathfrak{p}}\cup R^{\mathrm{im}}_{\mathfrak{k}}.

Let

\mathfrak{c}
denote the orthogonal to
\mathfrak{i}\oplus\mathfrak{r}
in
\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}
. Again
\mathfrak{c}
splits as

(3.27)

\mathfrak{c}=\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{p}}\oplus\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{k}}.

Moreover, we have the orthogonal splitting

(3.28)

\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}=\mathfrak{i}\oplus\mathfrak{r}\oplus\mathfrak{c}.

Proposition 3.6
.

The following identity holds:

(3.29)

\mathfrak{c}_{\mathbf{C}}=\oplus_{\alpha\in R^{\mathrm{c}}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}.

Proof.

This follows from equations (
3.10
),
(
3.23
), and (
3.28
).
∎

Now we give a result taken from
[
W88
, Lemma 2.3.5]
.

Proposition 3.7
.

A
\theta
-stable Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
is fundamental
if and only if there are no
real roots.

Proof.

If
\alpha\in R
, then
\alpha\in R^{\mathrm{re}}
if and only if
when
f\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
,

(3.30)

\left[\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}},f\right]=0.

If
\mathfrak{h}
is fundamental, by (
3.4
), then
f\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}
, so that
f=0
, which proves there are no real
roots. Put

(3.31)

\mathfrak{z}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}\right)=\left\{f\in\mathfrak{g},\left[\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}},f\right]=0\right\}.

Then
\mathfrak{z}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}\right)
is a Lie
subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{z}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}\right)
.
If
\mathfrak{h}
is not fundamental,
\mathfrak{z}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}\right)
is strictly larger than
\mathfrak{h}
, and there is a real root. The proof of our proposition is completed.
∎

If
\mathfrak{h}
is a Cartan subalgebra, the associated Cartan subgroup

H\subset G
is the stabilizer of
\mathfrak{h}
. Then
H
is a Lie
subgroup of
G
, with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}
.

Proposition 3.8
.

The vector spaces
\mathfrak{i}_{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{i}_{\mathfrak{k}},\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{k}}
have even dimension. Also

H\cap K
preserves these vector spaces, and
the corresponding determinants are equal to
1
. Also

\mathfrak{r}_{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r}_{\mathfrak{k}}
(resp.

\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{k}}
) have
the same dimension, and the actions of
H\cap K
on
these two vector spaces are equivalent. In particular, we have the
identity

(3.32)

\dim\mathfrak{p}-\dim\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{p}}=\dim\mathfrak{i}_{\mathfrak{p}}+\frac{1}{2}\dim\mathfrak{r}+\frac{1}{2}\dim\mathfrak{c}.

Proof.

If
\alpha\in R\setminus R^{\mathrm{re}}
, the
vanishing locus of
\alpha
in
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
is a
hyperplane. Therefore we can find
f_{\mathfrak{k}}\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}\setminus 0
such for any
\alpha\in R\setminus R^{\mathrm{re}}
,
\left\langle\alpha,f_{\mathfrak{k}}\right\rangle\neq 0
, which just says that

\mathrm{ad}\left(f_{\mathfrak{k}}\right)
acts as an invertible
endomorphism of
\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{c}
. This endomorphism preserves
their
\mathfrak{p}
and
\mathfrak{k}
components, and it is
classically antisymmetric. This is only possible if these vector
spaces are even-dimensional. If
k\in H\cap K
,
\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{-1}\right)
preserves
these vector spaces and commutes with
\mathrm{ad}\left(f_{\mathfrak{k}}\right)
. Therefore the eigenspaces associated with the eigenvalue

-1
are preserved by
\mathrm{ad}\left(f_{\mathfrak{k}}\right)
, so they
are even-dimensional. This forces the determinant of

\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{-1}\right)
to be equal to
1
on each of these
vector spaces.

We choose
b_{\mathfrak{p}}\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{p}}\setminus 0
such that for any
\alpha\in R\setminus R^{\mathrm{im}}
,
\left\langle\alpha,b_{\mathfrak{p}}\right\rangle\neq 0
. Therefore
\mathrm{ad}\left(b_{\mathfrak{p}}\right)
acts as an automorphism of

\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{c}
that
exchanges the corresponding
\mathfrak{p}
and
\mathfrak{k}

parts, and commutes with
\mathrm{Ad}\left(k^{-1}\right)
.

By (
3.28
), we get

(3.33)

\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}=\mathfrak{i}_{\mathfrak{p}}\oplus\mathfrak{r}_{\mathfrak{p}}\oplus\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{p}}.

Using the results we already established and (
3.33
), we get
(
3.32
). The proof of our proposition is completed.
∎

3.5.
A positive root system

Let
\mathfrak{h}
be a
\theta
-stable Cartan subalgebra, and let
R

denote the corresponding root system. Let
R_{+}\subset R
be a
positive root system. Set

(3.34)

\displaystyle R_{+}^{\mathrm{re}}=R_{+}\cap R^{\mathrm{re}},

\displaystyle R_{+}^{\mathrm{im}}=R_{+}\cap R^{\mathrm{im}},\qquad R^{\mathrm{c}}_{+}=R_{+}\cap R^{\mathrm{c}},

so that

(3.35)

R_{+}=R^{\mathrm{re}}_{+}\cup R_{+}^{\mathrm{im}}\cup R_{+}^{\mathrm{c}}.

In the whole paper, we choose
R_{+}
such that
-\theta

preserves

R_{+}\setminus R^{\mathrm{im}}_{+}
. Equivalently, we assume that if
\alpha\in R_{+}\setminus R^{\mathrm{im}}_{+}
, then

\overline{\alpha}\in R_{+}
.

Let us explain how to do this. If
\alpha\in R\setminus R^{\mathrm{im}}
, the vanishing locus of
\alpha
in
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a hyperplane, and so there is

b_{\mathfrak{p}}\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{p}},\left|b_{\mathfrak{p}}\right|=1
such that for any

\alpha\in R\setminus R^{\mathrm{im}}
,
\left\langle\alpha,b_{\mathfrak{p}}\right\rangle\neq 0
. The same argument shows
that there is
b_{\mathfrak{k}}\in h_{\mathfrak{k}},\left|b_{\mathfrak{k}}\right|=1
such that for
\alpha\in R^{\mathrm{im}}
,
\left\langle\alpha,b_{\mathfrak{k}}\right\rangle\neq 0
. For
\epsilon>0
,
b_{\pm}=\pm b_{\mathfrak{p}}+i\epsilon b_{\mathfrak{k}}\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{p}}\oplus i\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
, and

\theta
interchanges
b_{+}
and
b_{-}
. Also for
\epsilon>0

small enough, for
\alpha\in R
, the real numbers
\left\langle\alpha,b_{\pm}\right\rangle
do not vanish, and if
\alpha\in R\setminus R^{\mathrm{im}}
, they have opposite signs. Put

(3.36)

R_{+}=\left\{\alpha\in R,\left\langle\alpha,b_{+}\right\rangle>0\right\}.

Then
R_{+}
is a positive root system such that
-\theta
preserves

R_{+}\setminus R_{+}^{\mathrm{im}}
.

Note that
-\theta
acts without fixed points on

R^{\mathrm{c}}_{+}
, so that
\left|R^{\mathrm{c}}_{+}\right|
is even.

Definition 3.9
.

Put

(3.37)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{+,\mathbf{C}}=\oplus_{\alpha\in R^{\mathrm{c}}_{+}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{-,\mathbf{C}}=\oplus_{\alpha\in-R^{\mathrm{c}}_{+}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}.

Proposition 3.10
.

The vector spaces
\mathfrak{c}_{+,\mathbf{C}},\mathfrak{c}_{-,\mathbf{C}}
are the
complexifications of real Lie subalgebras
\mathfrak{c}_{+},\mathfrak{c}_{-}
of
\mathfrak{g}
, which have the same even dimension, and are such
that

(3.38)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}=\mathfrak{c}_{+}\oplus\mathfrak{c}_{-},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{-}=\theta\mathfrak{c}_{+}.

Also
B
vanishes on
\mathfrak{c}_{+},\mathfrak{c}_{-}
and induces
the identification,

(3.39)

\mathfrak{c}_{-}\simeq\mathfrak{c}_{+}^{*}.

The projections on
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{k}
map
\mathfrak{c}_{\pm}
into
\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{k}}

isomorphically. Finally, the actions of
H\cap K
on
\mathfrak{c}_{+},\mathfrak{c}_{-},\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{k}}
are equivalent.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.2
,
\mathfrak{c}_{+,\mathbf{C}},\mathfrak{c}_{-,\mathbf{C}}
are stable by conjugation, and so they are
complexifications of real vector spaces
\mathfrak{c}_{+},\mathfrak{c}_{-}
. The fact that these are Lie subalgebras is
obvious. Since
\left|R_{+}^{\mathrm{c}}\right|
is
even, these vector spaces are even-dimensional, and also they have the same dimension. By
Proposition
3.2
,
\theta
induces an isomorphism
of
\mathfrak{c}_{+}
into
\mathfrak{c}_{-}
. Using the
considerations that follow (
3.9
), we find that
B

vanishes on
\mathfrak{c}_{+},\mathfrak{c}_{-}
, and we obtain
(
3.39
). By Proposition
3.8
,
\mathfrak{c}_{+},\mathfrak{c}_{-},\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{k}}
have the same even dimension. The projections on

\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{k}
are given respectively by

\frac{1}{2}\left(1\mp\theta\right)
. Since
\theta
exchanges

\mathfrak{c}_{+}
and
\mathfrak{c}_{-}
, they restrict to
isomorphisms on
\mathfrak{c}_{+},\mathfrak{c}_{-}
. By Proposition

3.8
, we know that the actions
H\cap K
on

\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{k}}
are
equivalent. Since the adjoint action of
H\cap K
commutes with

\theta
, the corresponding representations of
H\cap K
on
these vector spaces are equivalent. The proof of our proposition is completed. ∎

If
f\in\mathfrak{h}
, then

(3.40)

\det\mathrm{ad}\left(f\right)_{\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}}=\prod_{\alpha\in R}\left\langle\alpha,f\right\rangle.

Definition 3.11
.

Let

\pi^{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}}\in S^{\cdot}\left(\mathfrak{h}^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
be such that if
h\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbf{C}}
, then

(3.41)

\pi^{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}}\left(h\right)=\prod_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\left\langle\alpha,h\right\rangle.

By (
3.40
), if
f\in\mathfrak{h}
,

(3.42)

\det\mathrm{ad}\left(f\right)_{|_{\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}}}=\pi^{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}}\left(f\right)\pi^{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}}\left(-f\right).

Also
f\in\mathfrak{h}
is regular if and only if
\pi^{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}}\left(f\right)\neq 0
.

Proposition 3.12
.

The function
\pi^{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}}
vanishes
identically on
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
if and only if

\mathfrak{h}
is not fundamental.

Proof.

Assume that
\mathfrak{h}
is not fundamental. By Proposition

3.7
, there are real roots, and so there are real
positive roots. If
\alpha\in R^{\mathrm{re}}_{+}
, then

\alpha
vanishes on
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
, and so

\pi^{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}}
vanishes on
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
. Conversely, if
\pi^{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}}

vanishes on
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
, one of the
\alpha\in R_{+}
has to vanish identically on
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
,
so that
\alpha\in R^{\mathrm{re}}_{+}
, and
\mathfrak{h}
is
not fundamental. The proof of our proposition is completed.
∎

3.6.
The case when
\mathfrak{h}
is fundamental and the root
system of
\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}},\mathfrak{k}\right)

In this Subsection, we assume that
\mathfrak{h}
is a
\theta
-stable
fundamental Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
. By (
3.18
),
we get

(3.43)

R^{\mathrm{c}}=R\setminus R^{\mathrm{im}}.

By Proposition
3.4
,

\mathfrak{r}=0
. By (
3.28
), we have the orthogonal
splitting,

(3.44)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}_{\mathfrak{p}}=\mathfrak{i}_{\mathfrak{p}}\oplus\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{p}},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}_{\mathfrak{k}}=\mathfrak{i}_{\mathfrak{k}}\oplus\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{k}}.

Also
\mathfrak{i}_{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{i}_{\mathfrak{k}}
have even
dimension,
\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{k}}

have the same even dimension, and the action of
H\cap K
on these
last two vector spaces are conjugate.

The roots in
R
do not vanish identically on
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
. We will now reinforce the choice of positive roots made in
Subsection
3.5
. We may and we will assume that

b_{\mathfrak{k}}\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}},\left|b_{\mathfrak{k}}\right|=1
has been chosen so that if
\alpha\in R
,
\left\langle\alpha,b_{\mathfrak{k}}\right\rangle\neq 0
.

As we saw in Subsection
3.5
,
-\theta
acts without
fixed points on
R^{\mathrm{c}}_{+}
. Also if

\alpha\in R^{\mathrm{c}}_{+}
,
-\theta\alpha|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}}=-\alpha|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}}
, so that the nonzero real numbers
\left\langle\alpha,ib_{\mathfrak{k}}\right\rangle
and
\left\langle-\theta\alpha,ib_{\mathfrak{k}}\right\rangle
have opposite signs.

Set

(3.45)

\displaystyle R^{\mathrm{im}}_{\mathfrak{k},+}=R^{\mathrm{im}}_{\mathfrak{k}}\cap R_{+},

\displaystyle R^{\mathrm{c}}_{++}=\left\{\alpha\in R^{\mathrm{c}}_{+},\left\langle\alpha,ib_{\mathfrak{k}}\right\rangle>0\right\}.

Definition 3.13
.

Let

R\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}},\mathfrak{k}\right)
be the
root system associated with the pair
\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}},\mathfrak{k}\right)
.
If

R_{+}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}},\mathfrak{k}\right)
is a
positive root system for
\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}},\mathfrak{k}\right)
, if
h_{\mathfrak{k}}\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k},\mathbf{C}}
, put

(3.46)

\pi^{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}},\mathfrak{k}}\left(h_{\mathfrak{k}}\right)=\prod_{\beta\in R_{+}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}},\mathfrak{k}\right)}\left\langle\beta,h_{\mathfrak{k}}\right\rangle.

Then
\left[\pi^{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}},\mathfrak{k}}\right]^{2}\left(h_{\mathfrak{k}}\right)

does not depend on the choice of
R_{+}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}},\mathfrak{k}\right)
. The arguments above (
3.45
) show that if
h\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
,
then

(3.47)

\prod_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\mathrm{c}}}\left\langle\alpha,h_{\mathfrak{k}}\right\rangle\geq 0.

Proposition 3.14
.

The map
\alpha\in R_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\mathrm{im}}\cup R^{\mathrm{c}}_{+}\to\alpha|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}}
is injective, and gives the identification

(3.48)

R\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}},\mathfrak{k}\right)=R^{\mathrm{im}}_{\mathfrak{k}}\cup R^{\mathrm{c}}_{+}.

A positive root system
R_{+}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}},\mathfrak{k}\right)
for
\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}},\mathfrak{k}\right)
is given by

(3.49)

R_{+}\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}},\mathfrak{k}\right)=R_{\mathfrak{k},+}^{\mathrm{im}}\cup R_{++}^{\mathrm{c}}.

If
h_{\mathfrak{k}}\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k},\mathbf{C}}
, then

(3.50)

\left[\pi^{h_{\mathfrak{k}},\mathfrak{k}}\left(h_{\mathfrak{k}}\right)\right]^{2}=\left(-1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}\left|R_{+}^{\mathrm{c}}\right|}\left[\prod_{\alpha\in R^{\mathrm{im}}_{\mathfrak{k},+}}\left\langle\alpha,h_{\mathfrak{k}}\right\rangle\right]^{2}\prod_{\alpha\in R_{+}^{\mathrm{c}}}\left\langle\alpha,h_{\mathfrak{k}}\right\rangle.

Proof.

By (
3.28
), we get

(3.51)

\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\perp}=\mathfrak{i}_{\mathfrak{k}}\oplus\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{k}},

and the above splitting is preserved by
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
.
The weights for this action on
\mathfrak{i}_{\mathfrak{k}}
are given
by
R^{\mathrm{im}}_{\mathfrak{k}}
. By Proposition
3.10
,

\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{k}}
and
\mathfrak{c}_{+}
are equivalent
under the action of
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
. By the first
equation in (
3.37
), the weights for the action of
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
on
\mathfrak{c}_{+}
are given by the restriction of

R_{+}^{\mathrm{c}}
to
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
.
Since the weights for the action of
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
on

\mathfrak{c}_{\mathfrak{k}}
are nonzero and of multiplicity

1
,
the map
\alpha\in R_{\mathfrak{k}}^{\mathrm{im}}\cup R_{+}^{\mathrm{c}}\to\alpha|_{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}}
gives the identification in (
3.48
). By
(
3.48
), we get (
3.49
). Using (
3.46
) and
the above results, we get (
3.50
). The proof of our proposition is completed.
∎

Remark 3.15
.

The results contained in Proposition
3.14
will play an
important role in the proof of the limit results of Subsection

8.1
.

3.7.
Cartan subgroups and regular elements

Assume that
\mathfrak{h}
is
\theta
-stable.
Then
\theta
restricts to an involution of
H
, and (
3.3
)
is the corresponding Cartan splitting of
\mathfrak{h}
. Also
B
restricts to a
H
and
\theta
invariant
